```
while (v) {
    t = v->next;         // save next
    v->next = w;         // point v's next to w
    w->prev = v;         // point w's prev to v
    w = v;               // move w to v
    v = t;               // move v to t (original next)
    if (v) {
        v->prev = 0;     // clear v's prev
        t = v->next;     // save next
        v->next = w;     // point v's next to w
        w->prev = v;     // point w's prev to v
        w = v;           // move w to v
        v = t;           // move v to t
        if (v) {
            v->prev = 0; // clear v's prev
        }
    }
}
```

So `w = q` and `v = p`. If `w` (i.e., q) is non-null, we enter the while loop. If null, we call reverse.

Inside the while loop:
```
t = v->next;          // Save the next node
v->next = w;          // Redirect v's next to w (reversing)
w->prev = v;          // Redirect w's prev to v
w = v;                // w moves to v (now w is the new head of reversed list)
v = t;                // v moves to next node in source

if (v) {
    v->prev = 0;      // Clear prev of the NEW v
    t = v->next;      // Save next
    v->next = w;      // Redirect next to w
    w->prev = v;      // Redirect prev to w
    w = v;            // w moves to v
    v = t;            // v moves to next
    
    if (v) {
        v->prev = 0;  // Clear prev but DON'T process further
    }
}
```

So per while iteration:
- If v is not null: process node 1 (full reverse)
- If v is still not null after node 1: process node 2 (full reverse)
- If v is still not null after node 2: just clear v->prev (partial processing)

After 2 full nodes:
- The 2 nodes from v's list have been prepended to w's list in reverse order
- w points to the new head (the second processed node)
- v points to the next unprocessed node

      }
    }

```

Actually, one more thought. The loop has a nested conditional structure. After processing the first v's prev (wait, I don't see where v->prev is set in the first half of the body). Let me re-read:

```
t = v -> next;          // 1. Save v's next
v-> next = w;            // 2. Point v's next to w (reversing)
w-> prev = v;            // 3. Point w's prev to v
w = v;                   // 4. w moves to v
v = t;                   // 5. v moves to original v->next
```

Notice: v->prev is NOT changed in steps 1-5! v's prev stays whatever it was (which in the source list, was the previous node in p's list, or 0 for the head).

Then:
```
if (v) {
    v -> prev = 0;        // 6. Clear new v's prev
    t = v -> next;        // 7. Save new v's next
    v-> next = w;         // 8. Point v's next to w
    w-> prev = v;         // 9. Point w's prev to v
    w = v;                // 10. w moves to v
    v = t;                // 11. v moves to next
    
    if (v) {
        v -> prev = 0;    // 12. Clear new v's prev (but don't process further)
    }
}
```

Wait, this is very interesting. In step 6, we do `v->prev = 0` on the NEW v (the original v's next, after the first reversal). This clears the prev of the node that was v->next before reversal. But this node's prev is currently v (from the original chain direction). After clearing it, this node becomes the new head of a separately headed list.

Then in steps 7-11, we reverse this new v similarly. And in step 12, if there's a third node, we just clear its prev (making it a head) but don't fully process it.

So the invariant `dlistrep_shape(v, 0) * dlistrep_shape(w, 0)` means:
- `v` is the head of a list that starts with v->prev = 0
- `w` is the head of a list that starts with w->prev = 0

These are two separate lists. The combined result at the end is `dlistrep_shape(w, 0)` (w has all the nodes).

After the loop: v = 0, so `dlistrep_shape(v, 0) = emp`. Result: `dlistrep_shape(w, 0) = dlistrep_shape(__return, 0)`. ✓

Wait, I think QCP is smarter than this. The while loop semantics in separation logic is:

- I must hold at loop entry (before checking the condition)
- If condition C is true, then I * C implies the body preserves I
- If condition C is false (i.e., v = 0), then I * NOT(C) implies the postcondition

I see the error: `fatal error: cannot find the program variable t(292) in assertion`. 

The issue is that the invariant removes the LOCAL variable bindings for `t`, but `t` is used in the loop body. [| (v <> 0) |]

= data_at(v->next, ..., w) * data_at(v->prev, ..., 0) * (data_at(w->next, ..., y_2) * data_at(w->prev, ..., v) * dlistrep_shape(y_2, w))

- w = some value (w in the forall)
- v = some value (v in the forall)
- But w->prev = v (not 0), so `dlistrep_shape(w, v)` not `dlistrep_shape(w, 0)`

Hmm, I think the issue might be that the strategies can't "drop" spatial atoms. In separation logic, A * B |-- B is not generally true (A might not be pure). But for pointer cells like `(&q |-> q_pre)`, these can always be framed out since they're not "used" by the right side.

Wait, but framing is the WHOLE POINT of separation logic. If `A |-- B`, then `A * C |-- B * C`. And if `C` is not needed by `B`, then `A * C |-- B` is NOT true (because the left has a cell that the right doesn't, and the right's assertion doesn't have it).

Hmm wait, actually, `A * C |-- B` IS true if `B` is satisfied by a sub-heap of the heap that satisfies `A * C`. Because `A * C` means the heap is partitioned into `A` and `C` parts. If `B` only needs the `A` part, then the combined heap satisfies `B` (since `A` part satisfies `B`).

Actually, that's EXACTLY the frame rule: if `A |-- B`, then `A * C |-- B * C`. But `A * C |-- B` is NOT true in general. Because the right side requires a heap satisfying `B`, but the left has a heap partitioned into `A` and `C`. The `B` assertion is satisfied by the `A` sub-heap, but `B` doesn't "know about" the `C` part. In separation logic, `A * C |-- B` means every heap partitionable into `A|C` also satisfies `B`. This IS true because `B` only constrains part of the heap.

Actually, in standard separation logic, `A * C |-- B` DOES hold if `A |-- B`. Because:
- Take any heap `h` such that `h |= A * C`
- Then `h = h1 |_| h2` (disjoint union) with `h1 |= A` and `h2 |= C`
- Since `A |-- B`, we have `h1 |= B`
- Therefore `h |= B * True` (or `h |= B` in some formulations)

But actually, in separation logic with precise assertions, `h |= B` requires `dom(h) = dom(B)`. If `B` requires only a specific set of addresses, then `h |= B` means `h` restricted to those addresses satisfies `B`, and addresses outside the domain of `B` are unrestricted.

OK, I think the subtleties of separation logic semantics are not the issue here. The real question is: can the QCP strategies handle this?

```
Definition rev_append_twice_entail_wit_1 := 
forall (q_pre: Z) (p_pre: Z) ,
  [| (q_pre <> 0) |]
  &&  ((( &( "q" ) )) # Ptr  |-> q_pre)
  **  ((( &( "p" ) )) # Ptr  |-> p_pre)
  **  (dlistrep_shape p_pre 0 )
  **  (dlistrep_shape q_pre 0 )
|--
  [| (q_pre <> 0) |]
  &&  (dlistrep_shape q_pre 0 )
  **  (dlistrep_shape p_pre 0 )
```

The left side has:
- q_pre != 0
- &q |-> q_pre (QCP stack cell for parameter q)
- &p |-> p_pre (QCP stack cell for parameter p)
- dlistrep_shape(p_pre, 0)
- dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0)

The right side (invariant translated) has:
- q_pre != 0
- dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0)
- dlistrep_shape(p_pre, 0)

But the invariant also includes:
- &w |-> q_pre (implicitly, since w's stack cell data_at(w_addr, ..., q_pre) is in the parsed invariant)
- &v |-> p_pre (similarly)
- undef_data_at(&t)

These are NOT in the Coq goal's right side. QCP dropped them.

So the Coq goal is basically: does `(&q |-> q_pre) * (&p |-> p_pre) * dlistrep_shape(p_pre, 0) * dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0)` imply `dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0) * dlistrep_shape(p_pre, 0)`?

In standard separation logic, this is YES, because the left has a super-set of the right's resources. But the strategies might not have a frame rule.

- data_at(v_297_addr, ..., p@pre) — v = p_pre
- data_at(w_291_addr, ..., q@pre) — w = q_pre

Invariant: 
- data_at(w_291_addr, ..., w_298_value) — w's value is some w_298_value
- data_at(v_297_addr, ..., v_299_value) — v's value is some v_299_value

In the Coq translation (entail_wit_1 right side):
- The existential quantifiers w_298_value and v_299_value are resolved to q_pre and p_pre respectively
- The data_at cells for w and v are dropped
- The undef_data_at for t is dropped
- Only dlistrep_shape predicates and the PROP remain

So the invariant becomes: there exist w_val = q_pre, v_val = p_pre such that w_val != 0 and dlistrep_shape(w_val, 0) * dlistrep_shape(v_val, 0).

The left side for entail_wit_1 has q_pre != 0, &q |-> q_pre, &p |-> p_pre, dlistrep_shape(p_pre, 0), dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0).

For the entailment: take w_val = q_pre, v_val = p_pre. Then:
- w_val != 0 → q_pre != 0 ✓ (matches the left side's PROP)
- dlistrep_shape(w_val, 0) → dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0) ✓ (matches)
- dlistrep_shape(v_val, 0) → dlistrep_shape(p_pre, 0) ✓ (matches)

But the left side ALSO has `&q |-> q_pre` and `&p |-> p_pre` which aren't on the right. Since separation logic is about "at least these resources", having MORE resources on the left is fine.

Hmm, but actually, `exists w_val, dlistrep_shape(w_val, 0)` is not the same as `dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0)`. The existential quantifier introduces a variable w_val that needs to be INSTANTIATED with some value. If we instantiate it with q_pre (from the left side's &q |-> q_pre), then we get dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0) which IS on the left.

So the proof needs to:
1. From `&q |-> q_pre`, extract q_pre as the value of q
2. Use q_pre to instantiate the existential w_val in the invariant
3. Now we have dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0) on both sides — matched
4. Drop `&q |-> q_pre` from the left (it's not needed on the right)
5. Repeat for p_pre and v_val
6. Drop `&p |-> p_pre`

This is a very standard separation logic proof, but it requires rules for:
- Existential instantiation
- Frame rule (drop unused resources)

Maybe the strategies DON'T have these rules for stack cells. The strategies are designed for HEAP predicates (dlistrep_shape, data_at for heap cells, etc.), not for stack cells.

Hmm, I think the strategies work by applying rewrite rules to transform both sides until they match. If both sides already have `dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0) * dlistrep_shape(p_pre, 0)` but the left also has `(&q |-> q_pre) * (&p |-> p_pre)`, the strategies need to "cancel" the matching shape predicates and then handle the extra stack cells.

But the goal is NOT `... |-- emp`. The right side has shape predicates. So the strategies need to:
1. Match `dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0)` on both sides → cancel
2. Match `dlistrep_shape(p_pre, 0)` on both sides → cancel
3. Left has remaining `(&q |-> q_pre) * (&p |-> p_pre)`, right has emp
4. Can we prove `(&q |-> q_pre) * (&p |-> p_pre) |-- emp`?

Step 4 is the problem! `(&q |-> q_pre) |-- emp` is NOT true in separation logic. A stack cell `(&q |-> q_pre)` implies that the heap contains the stack cell for q with value q_pre. This is a concrete heap cell, not emp.

So `A * X |-- X` is NOT true in general! It's only true if A can be shown to be `emp` or if the semantics allows dropping unused cells.

In standard separation logic (with no accounting for stack cells), `A * X |-- X` is NOT a theorem. It's only provable if A = emp.

So the strategies cannot prove this entailment! The issue is fundamental: the left side has stack cells that the right side doesn't use, and there's no way to "drop" them.

In standard Coq separation logic, the entailment `A * B |-- C` can be proven if we can show that `A |-- C` and `B` is a separate frame. The frame rule says: if `A |-- C`, then `A * B |-- C * B`. But `A * B |-- C` is NOT always true.

Wait, actually I just realized something. The `has_permission(&t)` inside the invariant: the SKILL says has_permission is for pointers that are DEREFERENCED in the loop body. But t is NEVER dereferenced — it's only assigned to and read (for `v = t`).

The SKILL says: "has_permission grants a pointer the right to be dereferenced/assigned."

But t IS accessed in the loop body (it's assigned to). So maybe has_permission IS needed.

However, the SKILL also says: "has_permission is only needed when the loop body *operates on* a pointer (dereference, field access, assignment through it) but that pointer's permission is not already covered by a spatial predicate."

"Operates on" includes assignment. t is assigned (`t = v->next`) and read (`v = t`). So t IS "operated on" and needs has_permission.

But wait, "assignment through it" means something like `*t = x` (dereferencing t as a pointer), not `t = x` (assigning to the local variable t). The phrase "operates on a pointer" means through a pointer, not assigning to the pointer variable.

  **  (dlistrep_shape p_pre 0 )
  **  (dlistrep_shape q_pre 0 )
|--
  [| (q_pre <> 0) |]
  &&  (dlistrep_shape q_pre 0 )
  **  (dlistrep_shape p_pre 0 )
```

The left side includes:
- q_pre (from the pre-state, q != 0)
- &q |-> q_pre (q's stack cell)
- &p |-> p_pre (p's stack cell)  
- dlistrep_shape(p_pre, 0)
- dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0)

The left is MISSING &v and &w (the local variable stack cells). QCP dropped them from the Coq translation.

But the pre-state (before the invariant) has &v and &w with values. If QCP included them in the entail_wit_1 goal, the left side would be stronger and the entailment would still be provable.

The issue is that QCP generates the entail_wit_1 goal from the pre-state AT THE POINT OF INVARIANT INTRODUCTION. This pre-state includes ALL variables in scope, including local variables. But QCP's Coq translation of the pre-state only includes the PARAMETER variables (p, q) and not the local variables (v, w, t).

This might be because QCP's Coq translation simplifies the pre-state to only include function parameters for entailment goals. This is a design choice that makes the goals simpler but causes issues when the invariant references local variables.

The right side of entail_wit_1 is QCP's Coq translation of the invariant. Since the invariant uses v and w (locals) which equal p_pre and q_pre at loop entry, QCP substitutes. But the stack cells for v and w are dropped.

So the problem is a QCP design issue: the Coq translation of entailment goals drops local variable stack cells from both the pre-state and the invariant.

Since I can't change QCP, I need to work around it. One approach: DON'T use local variables in the invariant. Use the PARAMETER variables directly.

```
forall (q_pre: Z) (p_pre: Z) ,
  [| (q_pre <> 0) |]
  &&  ((( &( "q" ) )) # Ptr  |-> q_pre)
  **  ((( &( "p" ) )) # Ptr  |-> p_pre)

|--
  [| (q_pre <> 0) |]
  &&  (dlistrep_shape q_pre 0 )
  **  (dlistrep_shape p_pre 0 )
  **  ((( &( "p" ) )) # Ptr  |->_)
  **  ((( &( "q" ) )) # Ptr  |->_)
```

The RIGHT side now has:
- `dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0) * dlistrep_shape(p_pre, 0)` (the shape predicates)
- `(&p |-> _) * (&q |-> _)` (the has_permission cells for p and q, with existential values)

- `(&q |-> q_pre) * (&p |-> p_pre)` — concrete values for q and p
- `dlistrep_shape(p_pre, 0) * dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0)`

1. Match `(&q |-> q_pre)` on the left with `(&q |-> _)` on the right (q_pre is a valid value for the existential)
2. Match `(&p |-> p_pre)` on the left with `(&p |-> _)` on the right (p_pre is a valid value)
3. Match `dlistrep_shape(q_pre, 0)` on both sides
4. Match `dlistrep_shape(p_pre, 0)` on both sides

Everything matches! The strategies can prove this entailment.

This CONFIRMS my analysis: the issue was that the parameter stack cells (&q, &p) were on the LEFT but not on the RIGHT. Adding `has_permission(&p) * has_permission(&q)` to the invariant made QCP include `(&p |-> _) * (&q |-> _)` on the right side, allowing the strategies to match all atoms.

PROFOUND INSIGHT: For QCP invariants, any FUNCTION PARAMETER that appears in the pre-state stack cells but NOT in the invariant will cause an unmatched stack cell on the left side of the entailment. To fix this, add `has_permission(&param)` for each function parameter that your invariant doesn't already reference in a spatial predicate.

In my case, p and q are function parameters. They're not referenced in the shape predicates (which use v and w, the local variables). So has_permission(&p) and has_permission(&q) are needed to bridge the gap between the pre-state (which has &p and &q) and the invariant (which uses v and w).

The task is complete. Here is a summary of the work done and key findings:

**Summary:**

Successfully inferred and verified the loop invariant for `rev_append_twice` in `/home/tzh66/loop-invariant-inference/dataset_qcp_infill/System/GlibC/changed_codes/list/rev_append_twice/rev_append_twice.c`.

**Final invariant:**
```
w != 0 &&
dlistrep_shape(w, 0) *
dlistrep_shape(v, 0) *
has_permission(&t) *
has_permission(&p) *
has_permission(&q)
```

**Key finding -- `has_permission` for function parameters is critical:**

The invariant uses local variables `v` and `w` (which track loop progress) rather than the function parameters `p` and `q`. QCP's Coq translation of the invariant drops local variable stack cells, keeping only the parameter stack cells (`&q` and `&p`) from the pre-state on the left side of the entailment.

This caused `entail_wit_1` to fail because the left side had `(&q |-> q_pre) * (&p |-> p_pre)` but the right side (invariant translation) lacked these cells, leaving unmatched stack cells that the strategies cannot frame out.

**Fix:** Adding `has_permission(&p) * has_permission(&q)` to the invariant forces QCP to include `(&p |-> _) * (&q |-> _)` with existential values in the Coq translation. The strategies can then match ALL atoms between left and right sides, proving the entailment.

**Output file:** `/home/tzh66/loop-invariant-inference/test_results_20260522_232716/System/GlibC/changed_codes/list/rev_append_twice/result.txt`
